Ineffectiveness for Search and Undecidability of PCSP Meta-Problems
Alberto Larrauri

Abstract
It is an open question whether the search and decision versions of promise CSPs are equivalent. Most known algorithms for PCSPs solve only their decision variant, and it is unknown whether they can be adapted to solve search as well. The main approaches, called BLP , AIP \text{BLP},\text{AIP} and BLP + AIP \text{BLP}+\text{AIP} , handle a PCSP by finding a solution to a relaxation of some integer program. We prove that rounding those solutions to a proper search certificate can be as hard as any problem in the class TFNP. In other words, these algorithms are ineffective for search. Building on the algebraic approach to PCSPs, we find sufficient conditions that imply ineffectiveness for search. Our tools are tailored to algorithms that are characterized by minions in a suitable way, and can also be used to prove undecidability results for meta-problems. This way, we show that the families of templates solvable via BLP , AIP \text{BLP},\text{AIP} , and BLP + AIP \text{BLP}+\text{AIP} are undecidable. Using the same techniques we also analyze several algebraic conditions that are known to guarantee the tractability of finite-template CSPs. We prove that several meta-problems related to cyclic polymorphims and WNUs are undecidable for PCSPs. In particular, there is no algorithm deciding whether a finite PCSP template (1) admits cyclic a polymorphism, (2) admits a WNU.



1
Introduction

The Dichotomy Theorem
[
65
,
25
]
and, more generally, the algebraic approach to constraint satisfaction
[
49
,
13
]
show that finite-template constraint satisfaction problems (CSPs) form a particularly well-behaved class of NP problems. This class includes a wide range of natural problems relevant across many domains, such as variants of the Boolean satisfiability problem, graph coloring problems, and systems of equations over finite algebraic structures. Unconditionally, the search and decision variant of each finite-template CSPs are polynomial-time equivalent. Each problem in this class is either NP-complete or in P, and there is an explicit procedure that correctly assigns one of these two cases to each given CSP.
This stands in contrast to the well-known fact that there are NP-intermediate problems if P
\neq
NP

[
52
]
, and there are problems in FNP (the search analogue of NP) which we believe to be hard but whose decision variants are trivial, captured by the TFNP class
[
40
]
. Therefore, it is natural to ask how far (and in which directions) can finite-template CSPs be generalized while still keeping their nice properties.

Promise
CSPs (PCSPs) are qualitative relaxations of CSPs generalizing the task of coloring a
c
-colorable graph using
d
colors for fixed integers
d\geq c
, called the
approximate graph coloring
(AGC) problem. A PCSP is given by a pair of relational structures
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, the
template
, where the first maps homomorphically into the second, denoted
{\bm{A}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. In the decision variant of
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
the task is to distinguish input structures
{\bm{I}}
satisfying
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{A}}
from those satisfying
{\bm{I}}\not\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. In the search variant, the goal is to find an explicit homomorphism from
{\bm{I}}
to
{\bm{B}}
given the promise that
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{A}}
. The algebraic approach, instrumental in much of the CSP theory and both proofs of the Dichotomy Theorem, has recently been extended to the PCSP framework
[
9
]
. Roughly, this approach studies the complexity of
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
by analyzing the algebraic properties of the set
\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
of
homomorphisms
f:{\bm{A}}^{n}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
from some direct power of
{\bm{A}}
to
{\bm{B}}
, called
polymorphisms
. This extension has motivated a surge of activity in the area that has yielded partial complexity classifications
[
21
,
8
,
37
,
58
,
17
,
54
,
59
]
, efficient algorithms
[
9
,
19
,
27
,
30
]
, and hardness conditions
[
22
,
12
,
50
,
7
]
. However, many basic questions remain unanswered. For example, the complexity classifications of Boolean and graph PCSPs (including the complexity of AGC) are still open, despite relevant progress in those directions
[
36
,
50
]
. Crucially, the relationship between search and decision PCSPs is not well understood, and it is unknown whether there is always an efficient way of solving a finite-template search PCSP whose decision variant is tractable.

In this work we analyze several PCSP algorithms, as well as some algebraic conditions that guarantee tractability in the CSP setting. It is known that, unless P=NP, there are tractable finite-template PCSPs which cannot be reduced via gadgets to a tractable finite-template CSP, as exemplified by “
1
-in-
3
-SAT vs not-all-equal-SAT”
[
15
,
2
,
57
,
61
]
. Therefore, there is a need to develop algorithms that go beyond the finite CSP case. Solving the decision variant of
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
involves solving a tractable relaxation of
\csp({\bm{A}})
which should be a restriction of
\csp({\bm{B}})
. The main relaxations used for this purpose are direct relaxations of the basic integer programming formulation of
\csp({\bm{A}})

[
9
,
19
,
18
]
, the local consistency algorithm
[
10
,
4
]
, and algorithmic hierarchies built on top of these two previous approaches
[
28
,
27
,
30
]
. Virtually all these algorithms present two inherent limitations. The first is that they only solve the decision version of
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
: when an instance
{\bm{I}}
is accepted by the relaxation, we do not directly obtain a homomorphism
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. To obtain such a homomorphism we must
round
the solution to the relaxation, and there is no obvious efficient way of doing so except for in a few known cases
[
21
,
20
]
. The second limitation of these algorithms is that, despite some of them admitting nice algebraic characterizations, we do not know of a way to recognize the problems
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
that they solve. This is known as the
meta-problem
related to these algorithms.

In our analysis of algorithms we focus on the
affine integer programming
(AIP) and the
basic linear programming
(BLP) relaxations for PCSPs, as well as on the
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
relaxation, which combines the power of the previous two. We present results of three kinds: hardness, undecidability, and non-computability. We show that rounding the output of these algorithms to search certificates can be as hard as any problem in the
total
FNP (TFNP) class, and that all these algorithms have undecidable meta-problems.

Theorem 1.1

(Main algorithmic result, informal)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}\in\{\text{AIP},\text{BLP},\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}\}
, and let
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{Q}}
be the family of finite templates
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
such that
\mathcal{Q}
solves
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
. Then, given any problem
\Lambda
in the TFNP class, there is a finite template
({\bm{A}}_{\Lambda},{\bm{B}}_{\Lambda})\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{Q}}
such that
\Lambda
is many-one reducible in polynomial time to the problem of finding a homomorphism
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}_{\Lambda}
for an input structure
{\bm{I}}
accepted by
\mathcal{Q}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{Q}}
is undecidable.

This is the first
hardness-of-rounding
result in the PCSPs literature.
If TFNP contains some problem that has no polynomial-time solution (which is widely conjectured
[
55
,
45
]
), then this gives a negative answer to the question of whether the output of these algorithms can always be rounded to a search solution efficiently, posed in
[
51
]

1
1

1

A previous version of this work also claimed the results presented here yielded negative answers to the questions in
[
20
,
19
]
, without further clarification. This was incorrect and misleading.
.
Furthermore, for
AIP
and
BLP
we show that there are finite-template PCSPs solved by those algorithms where no computable function is a valid rounding rule. This remains true even for
left-Boolean
templates
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
(i.e., those where
{\bm{A}}
’s domain contains only two elements). This means that directly rounding the output of these algorithms through consistent families of well-behaved polymorphisms such as the ones defined in
[
20
]
is not always possible. However, changing the domain of our relaxations to other
efficiently computable rings
, as proposed in
[
20
]
, could be a way to bypass the issues presented in this paper. This is expanded upon in the discussion section (
Section

10
).
We also point out that while our results include small bounds on
{\bm{A}}
’s domain size, the domain of the right structure
{\bm{B}}
can grow quite large. Hence, our results do not rule out the possibility that
\text{BLP},\text{AIP}
, and
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
could always be directly adapted for search in the Boolean setting.

Our techniques leverage the characterizations of the algorithms
AIP
,
BLP
, and
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
by means of objects called
minions
(or minor-closed classes
[
62
]
) that lie at the heart of the algebraic method. On a high level, our results follow from reducing tiling problems (e.g.,
[
43
]
) to
promise minor condition (
\mathrm{PMC}
) problems

[
9
]
.
We remark that, although the connection between the TFNP class and tiling problems is not difficult to prove, to our knowledge it has not been previously used to obtain hardness results within TFNP.
To achieve our reductions we develop a new way of encoding PCSPs in
\mathrm{PMC}
problems by means of a sheaf-like
[
23
]
construction on minions where elements represent partial homomorphisms, and minoring operations ensure consistency between the corresponding local homomorphisms.

We also study some well-known algebraic conditions following the same approach as in our analysis of algorithms. A polymorphism
f:{\bm{A}}^{n}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
is called
cyclic
if it is invariant under any cyclic permutation of its arguments, and
weak near-unanimity
(WNU) if the value of
f(x,\dots,x,y,x,\dots,x)
is independent of the position of the lone
y
. The existence of a WNU and the existence of a cyclic polymorphism are equivalent conditions that characterize the tractability of finite-template CSPs if P
\neq
NP
[
25
,
65
]
.
There is a rich network of equivalences between algebraic conditions in this setting (e.g.,
[
13
, Theorems 41 and 47]
) which were established using tools from universal algebra. These equivalences are a powerful aid in proving the decidability of various
meta-questions
. For instance, it is known that a finite structure
{\bm{A}}
admits a cyclic polymorphism if and only if it admits a cyclic polymorphism of every prime arity larger than the domain size of
{\bm{A}}

[
11
]
, and hence one can easily decide the existence of a WNU or a cyclic polymorphism. Another important algebraic condition is the presence of WNUs of all arities
k\geq 3
, which characterizes bounded width CSPs in the finite-template setting
[
10
,
48
]
. This condition is equivalent to the presence of WNUs of arities
3
and
4
satisfying a particular relation
[
48
]
and is, hence, decidable. The complexity of these and other meta-questions has been examined in
[
26
]
.

For PCSPs the picture is significantly less structured. We know that, unless P
\neq
NP, no finite family of polymorphisms can guarantee tractability
[
9
]
. Hence, all algebraic tractability conditions must involve an infinite family of polymorphisms. Even then, admitting WNUs of all arities
k\geq 3
is no longer a sufficient condition for bounded width (but remains a necessary one)
[
4
]
, and does not even guarantee tractability
[
29
]
. Since many of the tools from universal algebra no longer apply to the PCSP setting, few non-trivial implications between algebraic conditions have been shown (with some exceptions, e.g.
[
19
, Theorem 4]
). In particular, the decidability of most meta-questions related to natural algebraic conditions remains open for PCSPs. Our main algebraic result is the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

The following problems are undecidable. Given an input finite template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
with
|A|\leq 3
, determine whether
\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
contains:

(1)

cyclic polymorphisms

(i)

for every prime arity
p
,

(ii)

for at least one arity
k
,

(iii)

for all but finitely many prime arities
p
,

(iv)

for infinitely many prime arities
p
.

(2)

weak near‑unanimity polymorphisms (WNUs)

(i)

for every arity
k\geq 3
,

(ii)

for at least one arity
k\geq 3
,

(iii)

for all but finitely many arities
k\geq 3
,

(iv)

for infinitely many arities
k\geq 3
.

2
Preliminaries

The set of natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
starts at
1
, and
[k]
is the set
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. Given sets
S,T
, the set of maps
f:S\rightarrow T
is denoted by
T^{S}
.
A
partial function
from
S
to
T
is denoted as
f:S\partialmap T
.
We write
\id_{X}
for the identity map on a set
X
. We identify tuples
\bm{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in T^{n}
with functions in
T^{[n]}
in the natural way (i.e.,
\bm{t}(i)=t_{i}
).
Disjoint unions are denoted using
\sqcup
. We write
U^{*}
for the set of finite strings over a finite alphabet
U
, i.e.
U^{*}=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}U^{n}
. A partial map
f:U^{*}\partialmap U^{*}
is said to be
computable
if there is a Turing machine on an alphabet containing
U
that computes
f(\bm{x})
for any input
\bm{x}\in U^{*}
where
f
is defined, and runs forever on
\bm{x}\in U^{*}
where
f
is undefined. We informally say that a partial map
f:S\partialmap T
between countable sets
S,T
is computable if
f
is computable under some implicit encoding. That is, we implicitly refer to a finite alphabet
U
, and to injective maps
\alpha:S\rightarrow U^{*}
and
\beta:T\rightarrow U^{*}
, and mean that the partial map
\beta\circ f\circ\alpha^{-1}
is computable
[
31
]
.

Search Problems

We refer to
[
60
, Section 10.3]
for an introduction to search complexity and the classes FP, FNP, and TFNP.
Let
\mathfrak{R}\subseteq U^{*}\times V^{*}
be a binary relation on
finite words, and
\bot
a special “reject” symbol outside the alphabets
U,V
. The
search problem

\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
,
is defined as: given a string
\bm{x}\in U^{*}
,
find some
\bm{y}\in V^{*}
satisfying
(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in\mathfrak{R}
, or reject
\bm{x}
and return
\bot
if no such
\bm{y}
exists. The class of
total functional NP
(TFNP) problems consists of all problems
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
where
\mathfrak{R}
is (1) a total relation, meaning that for all input strings
\bm{x}
there is some
\bm{y}
satisfying
(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in\mathfrak{R}
, (2) polynomially bounded, meaning that there is some polynomial
p(n)
such that
(\bm{x},\bm{y})\in\mathfrak{R}
implies
|\bm{y}|\leq p(|\bm{x}|)
, and (3)
\mathfrak{R}
is recognizable in polynomial time. The class of
tally TFNP
problems, denoted TFNP
1
, consists of all problems
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}\in\text{TFNP}
such that
\mathfrak{R}\subseteq U^{*}\times V^{*}
is a relation where
U
is the unary alphabet
\{1\}
.

The class TFNP has been studied extensively (e.g.,
[
40
,
45
,
42
]
)
and contains several problems for which no efficient solution is expected such as integer factoring, or the problem of computing a Nash equilibrium
[
33
]
.
There is compelling reason to believe that
\text{TFNP}\not\subseteq\text{FP}

[
45
]
(FP being the class of search problems solvable in polynomial time), which would imply that
\text{P}\neq\text{NP}
.
On the other hand, there are oracles with respect to which
\text{TFNP}\subseteq\text{FP}
but the polynomial hierarchy is infinite
[
24
]
.
To our knowledge, the class TFNP
1
has not been studied explicitly, but also contains problems that have no known polynomial-time solution, such as
Prime
, where
1^{n}
is given as input and the task is to find a prime number on
n
bits
[
39
]
.

A (polynomial-time)
many-one reduction
from
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}
to
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}
consists of a pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of polynomial-time computable functions satisfying that for all
\bm{x}
(1)
if
(\alpha(\bm{x}),\bm{z})\in\mathfrak{R}_{2}
, then
(\bm{x},\beta(\bm{x},\bm{z}))\in\mathfrak{R}_{1}
,
and (2) if
(\alpha(\bm{x}),\bm{z})\not\in\mathfrak{R}_{2}
for all
\bm{z}
, then

(\bm{x},\bm{y})\notin\in\mathfrak{R}_{1}
for all
\bm{y}
. Similarly, a
generalized many-one reduction
from
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}
to
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}
is a pair of polynomial-time computable functions
(\alpha,\beta)
satisfying that whenever
\bm{z}
is a valid answer to
\alpha(\bm{x})
in the problem
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}_{2}}

(including the case where
\bm{z}=\bot
), then
\beta(\bm{x},\bm{z})
is a valid answer to
\bm{x}
in the problem
\mathfrak{R}_{1}
(including again the case where
\beta(\bm{x},\bm{z})=\bot
). Given a class
\mathcal{C}
of search problems, and a set
\mathcal{F}
of search problems, we say that
\mathcal{F}
is
\mathcal{C}
-hard if for any
\mathfrak{R}\in\mathcal{C}
there is some
\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
has a many-one reduction to
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}^{\prime}}
. A problem
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}
is called
\mathcal{C}
-hard if the family
\{\Lambda_{\mathfrak{R}}\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-hard. The class TFNP is conjectured to contain no TFNP-hard problems
[
55
]
, so our results show TFNP-hardness of families.

We also consider
promise
search problems, which can be seen as “partial” search problems. The notions in this section extend to promise problems in the natural way, along the lines of e.g.,
[
35
,
41
]
.

Promise Constraint Satisfaction

A
relational signature

\Sigma

is a finite set of symbols where each
R\in\Sigma
has some
arity

\mathrm{ar}(R)\in\mathbb{N}
. A
\Sigma
-
structure

{\bm{A}}
consists of: a set
A
called its
universe
, and a relation
R^{A}\subseteq A^{\mathrm{ar}(R)}
for each
R\in\Sigma
. Given two
similar
(i.e., with the same signature) structures
{\bm{A}},{\bm{B}}
, a homomorphism
h:{\bm{A}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
is a map from
A
to
B
satisfying
(h(e(1)),\dots,h(e(\mathrm{ar}(R))))\in R^{B}

for each
R\in\Sigma
and each tuple
e\in R^{A}
. We write
{\bm{A}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
to denote there is a homomorphism from
{\bm{A}}
to
{\bm{B}}
.
The
n
-th power
of
{\bm{A}}
, denoted
{\bm{A}}^{n}
, is a structure similar to
{\bm{A}}
whose universe is
A^{n}
, and where

R^{A^{n}}
consists of the tuples
(\bm{a}_{1},\dots,\bm{a}_{\mathrm{ar}(R)})
, satisfying
(a_{1,j},\dots a_{\mathrm{ar}(R),j})\in R^{A}
for all
j\in[n]
.

Templates
are pairs of structures
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
satisfying
{\bm{A}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. The template is

finite
if both
{\bm{A}},{\bm{B}}
are finite.
The
decision promise constraint satisfaction problem
(PCSP) defined by a template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, denoted

\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, is the problem of, given an input finite structure
{\bm{I}}
, to accept it
if
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{A}}
, and to reject it if
{\bm{I}}\not\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. Similarly, in the
search PCSP
defined by
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, denoted
\spcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, the goal is to find a homomorphism
F:{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
, if
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{A}}
, or to reject
{\bm{I}}
if
{\bm{I}}\not\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. Observe that this only makes sense if
{\bm{B}}
is finite or a suitable encoding is fixed. We define the problems
\csp({\bm{A}})
and
\scsp({\bm{A}})
as
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{A}})
and
\spcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{A}})
respectively.

Efficient Algorithms and Rounding Problems

Let
{\bm{A}}
,
{\bm{I}}
be finite
\Sigma
-structures. The following system of equations over the integers
\{0,1\}
, denoted
\text{IP}_{{\bm{A}}}({\bm{I}})
, is satisfiable if and only if
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{A}}
:

Variables:

\displaystyle\{x_{v,a}\,|\,v\in I,a\in A\}\quad\sqcup

\displaystyle\{x_{r_{A},r_{I}}\,|\,R\in\Sigma,r_{I}\in R^{I},r_{A}\in R^{A}\}.

Equations:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l r}\sum_{a\in A}x_{v,a}=1,\\
\sum_{r_{A}\in R^{A}}x_{r_{I},r_{A}}=1,\\
\sum_{r_{A}\in R^{A},r_{A}(i)=\alpha}x_{r_{I},r_{A}}=x_{r_{I}(i),\alpha},\end{array}

for each

v\in I
,
R\in\Sigma
,
r_{I}\in R^{I}
,
i\in[\mathrm{ar}(R)]
,
\alpha\in A
.

Solving this system is as difficult as solving
\csp({\bm{A}})
, but if we allow the variables to take arbitrary values in
\mathbb{Z}
or in the rational interval
[0,1]
then the task can be carried out in polynomial time. We write
\text{AIP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
and
\text{BLP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
for the system

\text{IP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
when the domain of the variables is
\mathbb{Z}
or
[0,1]\subseteq\mathbb{Q}
respectively.

Given an input structure
{\bm{I}}
for
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, where
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
is a finite template, the
AIP
algorithm (resp.,
BLP
)
[
9
]
solves
\text{AIP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
(resp.,
\text{BLP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
) and accepts
{\bm{I}}
if and only if this system is satisfiable. The algorithm
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}

[
19
]
combines the power of the previous two, and checks in polynomial time whether
\text{AIP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
and
\text{BLP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
have compatible solutions, and accepts
{\bm{I}}
when this is the case. Compatibility here means that whenever a variable is assigned to
0
in
\text{BLP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
, it is also assigned to
0
in
\text{AIP}_{\bm{A}}({\bm{I}})
. An algorithm
\mathcal{Q}\in\{\text{AIP},\text{BLP},\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}\}
solves
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
if it always outputs a correct answer in this problem, meaning that the following two implications hold: (1) if there is a homomorphism
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{A}}
then
\mathcal{Q}
accepts
{\bm{I}}
, and (2) if
\mathcal{Q}
accepts
{\bm{I}}
then there is a homomorphism
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
. In this situation we define the
rounding problem

\spcsp_{\mathcal{Q}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
as the problem of, given an instance
{\bm{I}}
accepted by
\mathcal{Q}
, to find a homomorphism from
{\bm{I}}
to
{\bm{B}}
.

Minions

A
minion

\mathscr{M}
consists of a collection of disjoint sets
\mathscr{M}(n)
indexed by the natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and a map
\pi^{\mathscr{M}}:\mathscr{M}(n)\rightarrow\mathscr{M}(m)

for each
pair of numbers
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and each function
\pi\in[m]^{[n]}
, satisfying that (1)

\pi^{\mathscr{M}}=\id_{\mathscr{M}(n)}
if
\pi=\id_{[n]}
, and (2)
\pi^{\mathscr{M}}=\pi_{1}^{\mathscr{M}}\circ\pi_{2}^{\mathscr{M}}

whenever
\pi=\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2}
. The elements
f\in\mathscr{M}(n)
are called
n
-ary
, the functions
\pi^{\mathscr{M}}
are called
minoring operations
, and an element
\pi^{\mathscr{M}}(f)
is called a
minor
of
f
. When
\mathscr{M}
is clear from the context, we write
f^{\pi}
instead of
\pi^{\mathscr{M}}(f)
. The minion
\mathscr{M}
is called
locally finite
if
\mathscr{M}(n)
is a finite set for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. An element
p\in\mathscr{M}(n)
is called
cyclic

if
p=p^{(n,1,2,\dots,n-1)}
(we remind the reader that we represent maps
\pi\in[n]^{[n]}
as tuples). A

weak near-unanimity
(WNU) element is some
p\in\mathscr{M}(n)
satisfying
that
p^{\sigma_{i}}=p^{\sigma_{j}}
for all
i,j\in[n]
, where
\sigma_{i}\in[2]^{[n]}
sends
i
to
1
and all other
o\in[n]
to
2
. Given a template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
, the
polymorphism minion

\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
is a minion whose
n
-ary elements are the homomorphisms
f:{\bm{A}}^{n}\rightarrow{\bm{B}}
, which are called
polymorphisms
. Given an
n
-ary polymorphism
f
, and a map
\pi:[n]\rightarrow[m]
, the minor
f^{\pi}
is defined by
f^{\pi}(\bm{a})=f(\bm{a}\circ\pi)

for every
f\in A^{n}
. Finally, a
minion homomorphism

F:\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}

is a map from elements of
\mathscr{M}
to elements of
\mathscr{N}
that preserves arities and minoring operations, i.e., satisfying that

F(f)^{\pi}=F(f^{\pi})

for each suitable
f,\pi
. Similarly, given
h\in\mathbb{N}
, a
partial homomorphism

F:\mathscr{M}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{
$\xrightharpoonup{\penalty\ h\penalty\ }$}}\mathscr{N}

up to arity
h
is a partial map defined on all elements
f\in\mathscr{M}
of arity at most
h
that preserves arities and minoring operations.

We define three minions that characterize the power of the algorithms
\text{AIP},\text{BLP},\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
.
In the minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
the
n
-ary elements are the tuples
f\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

satisfying
\sum_{i\in[n]}f(i)=1
.
Given
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}(n)
, and
\pi\in[m]^{[n]}
, minoring is defined by the identity
(f^{\pi})(i)=\sum_{j\in\pi^{-1}(i)}f(j)
for each
i\in[m]
.
In the minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}}
the
n
-ary elements are the the tuples
f\in[0,1]^{n}
of rational numbers for which
\sum_{i\in[n]}f(i)=1
. Minoring is defined as for
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
, i.e., by the identity
(f^{\pi})(i)=\sum_{j\in\pi^{-1}(i)}f(j)
. Finally, in the minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}}
the
n
-ary elements are pairs
(f,g)
, where
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}}(n),g\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}(n)
, and
f(i)=0
implies
g(i)=0
for each
i\in[n]
.
Minoring in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}}
is defined component wise. That is,
(f,g)^{\pi}=(f^{\pi},g^{\pi})
, where
f^{\pi}=\pi^{\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}}}(f)
, and
g^{\pi}=\pi^{\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}}(g)
. The following theorem has been shown in
[
9
]
for the algorithms
\text{AIP},\text{BLP}
and in
[
19
]
for
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}\in\{\text{AIP},\text{BLP},\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}\}
, and let
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
be a finite template. Then the algorithm
\mathcal{Q}
solves
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
if and only if
\mathscr{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}\rightarrow\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
.

In (non-)computability results we consider the plain encoding of
\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}},\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}},\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}}
, where tuples are represented as comma separated lists, delimited with parentheses, integers are represented with their decimal representations, and rational numbers are represented as irreducible fractions, written as two numbers separated by a forward slash (i.e.,
n/m
).

Minor Conditions

Another useful way of looking at minions is to consider them as multi-sorted structures
[
32
]
, where the sort of each element
p\in\mathscr{M}
is its arity
\mathrm{ar}(p)
, and
\cdot^{\pi}
is a function from
m
-ary elements to
n
-ary elements for each map
\pi\in[n]^{[m]}
. We consider the multi-sorted first-order (FO) language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{MC}}

of minions. For some background of multi-sorted (or many-sorted) FO logic we refer to
[
38
]
, but we require only the very basics. Formulas in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{MC}}
are built using variables, each of which has an arity (i.e., its sort), Boolean connectives, and function symbols
\cdot^{\pi}
for each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and each map
\pi\in[m]^{[n]}
. Variables represent elements of minions, and each symbol
\cdot^{\pi}
represents the corresponding minoring operation. We write
\exists^{n}x
rather than
\exists x
to make explicit that
x
is a
n
-ary variable, and
\phi(x_{1}^{n_{1}},\dots,x_{k}^{n_{k}})
to express that the free variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
of the formula
\phi
have arities
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
respectively. For example, the formula

\exists^{3}x\left(x=x^{(2,3,1)}\right)
expresses the existence of a
3
-ary cyclic element. The
maximum arity
of a formula
\phi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{MC}}
is the largest maximum arity of any of its sub-terms. A
primitive positive formula
is one that does not include disjunction, negation, or universal quantification, and a
sentence
(or a
closed formula
) is a formula with no free variables.
Minor conditions
are closed pp-formulas in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{MC}}
. We remark that more commonly the notion of minor condition is introduced using bipartite Label Cover instances (e.g.,
[
9
]
).

Given a minion
\mathscr{M}
, a formula
\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{MC}}
, and elements
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}\in\mathscr{M}
such that

f_{i}
has the same arity as
x_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
, we write
\mathscr{M}\models\phi(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
to express that

\mathscr{M}
satisfies
\phi
when substituting each
x_{i}
with the element
f_{i}
. A
pp-definition
of a set
Q\subseteq\mathscr{M}
is a pp-formula
\Phi(x)
such that
\mathscr{M}\models\Phi(f)
if and only if
f\in Q
. Suppose that
\mathscr{M}\models\phi
for a minor condition
\phi
. A
satisfying assignment
of
\phi
in
\mathscr{M}
maps each occurrence of the existential quantifier
\exists x\varphi(x)
in
\phi
to an element
f
of
\mathscr{M}
of the same arity as
x
in such a way that
\phi
is satisfied after substituting
\exists x\varphi(x)
with
\varphi(f)
in each sub-formula. When there is no ambiguity, we will simply treat assignments as maps from variables of
\phi
to elements of
\mathscr{M}
. Given two minions
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}
and a number
h\in\mathbb{N}
, in the
promise minor condition problem

\mathrm{PMC}_{h}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{N})
we consider an input minor condition
\phi
whose maximum arity is at most
h
, and the task is to accept it if
\mathscr{M}\models\phi
and reject it if
\mathscr{N}\not\models\phi
. Additionally, if
\mathscr{N}
is locally finite, we define the
search promise minor condition problem

\mathrm{sPMC}_{h}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{N})
as the problem of either finding a satisfying assignment of
\phi
in
\mathscr{N}
or to reject
\phi
if
\mathscr{M}\not\models\phi
.

3
Main Results

We are ready to state our main results about the algorithms
\text{AIP},\text{BLP},
and
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
. These are summarized in
Figure

1
. Given
\mathcal{Q}\in\{\text{AIP},\text{BLP},\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}\}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}
denotes the family of finite templates
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
with
|A|\leq k
such that
\mathcal{Q}
solves
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
.

Theorem 3.1

(Main result for AIP)
.

The following hold:

(1)

the family of rounding problems
\spcsp_{\text{AIP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})

for
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{AIP}}_{4}
is TFNP-hard,

(2)

the family
\spcsp_{\text{AIP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})

for
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{AIP}}_{2}
is TFNP
1
-hard,

(3)

the family
\mathcal{S}^{\text{AIP}}_{2}
is undecidable, and

(4)

there is a template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{AIP}}_{2}

for which there is no computable minion homomorphism from
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
to
\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
.

Theorem 3.2

(Main result for BLP)
.

The following hold:

(1)

the family of rounding problems
\spcsp_{\text{BLP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})

for
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{BLP}}_{5}
is TFNP-hard,

(2)

the family
\spcsp_{\text{BLP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})

for
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{BLP}}_{2}
is TFNP
1
-hard,

(3)

the family
\mathcal{S}^{\text{BLP}}_{2}
is undecidable, and

(4)

there is a template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{BLP}}_{2}

for which there is no computable minion homomorphism from
\mathscr{M}_{\text{BLP}}
to
\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
.

Theorem 3.3

(Main result for
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
)
.

The following hold:

(1)

the family of rounding problems
\spcsp_{\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})

for
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}}_{5}
is TFNP-hard, and

(2)

the family
\mathcal{S}^{\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}}_{5}
is undecidable.

We also recall here our main result about cyclic polymorphisms and WNUs. We remark that
\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
admitting
some cyclic polymorphism is equivalent to it admitting one of some prime arity.

See
1.2

4
Overview of the Proofs

We sketch the proof of the following theorem while outlining the ideas used in our main results.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of finite templates
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
with
|A|\leq 3
such that
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
is solved by
AIP
. Then (1) the family of problems
\spcsp_{\text{AIP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
where
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}
is TFNP
1
-hard, (2)
\mathcal{S}
is undecidable, and (3) there is a template

({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})\in\mathcal{S}
for which there is no computable homomorphism
F:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
.

The proof of this result is a reduction from tiling problems. The signature
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
consists of a unary symbol
O
and binary symbols
E_{1},E_{2}
, and
\bm{\Gamma}
is the
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
-structure whose universe is the upper-right quadrant
\mathbb{N}^{2}
, and where
O^{\Gamma}=\{(1,1)\}
marks the origin, and
E_{1}^{\Gamma}=\{((m,n),(m+1,n))\,|\,(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\}
,
E_{2}^{\Gamma}=\{((m,n),(m,n+1))\,|\,(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\}

are the horizontal and vertical adjacency relations. We write
\Hom(\bm{\Gamma},\cdot)
for the set of finite structures
\bm{T}
satisfying
\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
. A
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
-structure
\bm{T}
can be seen as a set of tiles, equipped with horizontal and vertical constraints, and a set of distinguished tiles that can occupy the initial position. This way, a homomorphism
F:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
represents a tiling of the upper-right quadrant of the plane that satisfies all the imposed restrictions.

Proposition 4.2
.

The following hold:

(1)

the family of problems
\spcsp(\bm{\Gamma},\bm{T})
where
\bm{T}\in\Hom(\bm{\Gamma},\cdot)
is TFNP
1
-hard,

(2)

\Hom(\bm{\Gamma},\cdot)
is undecidable, and

(3)

there exists
\bm{T}\in\Hom(\bm{\Gamma},\cdot)
for which there is no computable homomorphism
F:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
.

At a high-level, this is follows from the fact that, given a non-deterministic Turing machine
M
, we can construct a finite structure
\bm{T}_{M}
for which the homomorphisms
F:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}_{M}
represent non-halting runs of
\bm{T}_{M}
.

In order to prove
Theorem

4.1
using this proposition we first represent
\bm{\Gamma}
inside the minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
, and then we find a way of encoding finite
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
-structures in finite templates
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
in a suitable way. The first task is the simpler one. We can identify each pair
\bm{m}=(m_{1},m_{2})
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
with the
3
-ary element
f_{\bm{m}}=(m_{1},m_{2},1-m_{1}-m_{2})\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
. Similarly, we can represent each pair
(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{\Gamma}
with the
4
-ary element
g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}=(m_{1},m_{2},1,-m_{1}-m_{2})
with the idea that
f_{\bm{m}}=g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}^{(1,2,3,3)}
and

f_{\bm{m}^{\prime}}=g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}^{(1,2,i,3)}
in mind. We call this way of representing a relational structure inside a minion an
interpretation
(
Section

6.1
) and we denote it by
\mathcal{I}
. We define
U^{\mathcal{I}}=\{f_{\bm{m}}\,|\,\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\}
,
O^{\mathcal{I}}=\{f_{(1,1)}\}
, and
E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}=\{g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}\,|\,(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{\Gamma}\}
for
i=1,2
.

The interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is
almost
pp-definable in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
. Indeed, the element
(1,0)\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}(2)
is the only witness of the pp-formula
\phi_{1}(x^{2})\equiv x=x^{(1,1)}
, so the
4
-ary elements of the form
(m_{1},m_{2},1,-m_{1}-m_{2})\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
are precisely the witnesses of
\phi_{E}(y^{4})\equiv\phi_{1}(y^{(2,2,1,2)})
. However, this does not take into account the restriction that
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
. Under closer inspection it is not hard to see that the set of elements
(m,n)\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
with
m>0
is not pp-definable, so, in fact, the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
itself is not pp-definable either
2
2

2

This could be circumvented by having defined
\bm{\Gamma}
on the whole integer plane
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
instead of
\mathbb{N}^{2}
, but we want to illustrate the point that our reductions are not uniform pp-definitions. This will be useful in the more involved proofs.
. However, we get something almost as good: for any element
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
we can construct in polynomial time a pp-definition
\psi_{\bm{m}}(x^{3})
of
f_{\bm{m}}
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
. For
\bm{m}=(1,1)
, we simply define
\psi_{\bm{m}}(x^{3})\equiv\phi_{1}(x^{(1,2,2)})\wedge\phi_{1}(x^{(2,1,2)})
.
Given
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for
\bm{m}^{\prime}=(m_{1}+1,m_{2})
, we define

\psi_{\bm{m}^{\prime}}(x^{3})
as the formula

\bigexists^{4}y\bigexists^{3}z\left(\psi_{\bm{m}}(z)\wedge\phi_{E}(y)\wedge z=y^{(1,2,3,3)}\wedge x=y^{(1,2,1,3)}\right),

(4)

and so on. This way, given a finite substructure
\bm{G}\subset\bm{\Gamma}
, we can construct in polynomial time a minor condition
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
containing an existentially-quantified variable
x_{\bm{m}}
for each vertex
\bm{m}\in G
an existentially-quantified variable
y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}
for each edge
(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{G}
, such that the satisfying assignments of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
on
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
must map
each variable to the minion element that encodes the corresponding vertex or edge. That is,

x_{\bm{m}}\mapsto f_{\bm{m}}
and

y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}\mapsto g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}
. The minor condition
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
can be defined as

\bigexists_{\bm{m}\in G}^{3}x_{\bm{m}}\bigexists_{i\in[2],(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{G}}^{4}y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}\left(\bigwedge_{\bm{m}\in G}\psi_{\bm{m}}(x_{\bm{m}})\right)\bigwedge\\
\Biggl(\bigwedge_{i\in[2],(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{G}}\psi_{E}(y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})})\wedge x_{\bm{m}}=y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}^{(1,2,3,3)}\wedge x_{\bm{m}^{\prime}}=y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}^{(1,2,i,3)}\Biggr).

(5)

This construction is called a
pattern
(
Section

6.4
). Having found a nice way to represent
\bm{\Gamma}
inside
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
, the next step is to develop a construction that, given a finite
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
-structure
\bm{T}
, builds a suitable finite template
({\bm{A}}_{\bm{T}},{\bm{B}}_{\bm{T}})
. We would like
\mathscr{M}_{\bm{T}}=\pol({\bm{A}}_{\bm{T}},{\bm{B}}_{\bm{T}})
to satisfy the following.

Property 1

(Conditions that enable our reductions)
.

(I)

\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{\bm{T}}
if and only if
\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
.

(II)

A homomorphism
F:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
can be computed given oracle access to a homomorphism
H:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{\bm{T}}
.

(III)

Given a finite substructure
\bm{G}\subset\bm{\Gamma}
, there is a
polynomial-time reduction from the task of finding a homomorphism
F:\bm{G}\rightarrow\bm{T}

to the task of finding a satisfying assignment of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
in
\mathscr{M}_{\bm{T}}
.

It is easy enough to see that the conditions
(I)
and
(II)
allow us to respectively reduce the undecidability and non-computability parts of
Theorem

4.1
to those of
Proposition

4.2
. In to bridge the gap between the TFNP
1
-hardness statements in those results, we use condition
(III)
together with the following additional result, which is a consequence of
Theorem

8.5
(
Section

8
). We note that this is a standard modification of an analogous result,
[
9
, Theorem 3.12]
, that has been applied widely in the PCSP literature.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose that
AIP
solves
\pcsp({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
for a finite template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
. Then
\spcsp_{\text{AIP}}({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
is log-space equivalent to
\mathrm{sPMC}_{N}(\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}},\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}}))
, where
N
is at least as large as
|A|
and
|R^{A}|
for all relation symbols
R
.

We remark that we do not always achieve the conditions from
Property

1
in tandem: for example, sometimes we obtain templates that satisfy both
(I)
and

(III)
, but not
(II)
, meaning that we are able to obtain undecidability, and hardness-of-rounding results for a given algorithm, but not a related non-computability result. This is the case, for example, of
\text{BLP}+\text{AIP}
. With our methods, the condition
(I)
used to prove undecidability has the weakest requirements, while the conditions
(II)
and
(III)
used to show non-computability and hardness have non-comparable requirements.

Rather than constructing the template
({\bm{A}}_{\bm{T}},{\bm{B}}_{\bm{T}})
directly, we focus on its polymorphism minion instead.
We start by defining a minion
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
that satisfies
Property

1
of
\pol({\bm{A}}_{\bm{T}},{\bm{B}}_{\bm{T}})
. The
n
-ary elements of
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
are pairs
(f,\chi)
, where
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}(n)
, and
\chi:[3]^{[n]}\partialmap T
is a partial map
defined on the elements
\gamma\in[3]^{[n]}
such that
f^{\gamma}\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
that satisfies the following properties: if
f_{(1,1)}=f^{\gamma}
, then
\chi(\gamma)\in O^{T}
, and if
g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}=f^{\pi}
, then

(\chi(\gamma),\chi(\gamma^{\prime}))\in E_{i}^{T}
, where
\gamma=(1,2,3,3)\circ\pi
, and
\gamma^{\prime}=(1,2,i,3)\circ\pi
. Intuitively, the element
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}

covers
some part of
\bm{\Gamma}
(as interpreted by
\mathcal{I}
), which consists of the elements
f_{\bm{m}}\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
that can be obtained by minoring
f
. Similarly, we also think of
f
as covering the edges
(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{\Gamma}
where
g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}\in E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
is a minor of
f
. Then, the map
\chi
represents a homomorphism from the substructure
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{f}\subseteq\bm{\Gamma}
covered by
f
to
\bm{T}
. Given a map
\pi\in[m]^{[n]}
, we define the minor
(f,\chi)^{\pi}
as the pair
(f^{\pi},\chi^{\pi})
, where
\chi^{\pi}:[3]^{[m]}\partialmap T
is the partial map defined by
\gamma\mapsto\chi(\gamma\circ\pi)
. The rationale behind this construction is that the structure
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{f^{\pi}}
covered by
f^{\pi}
is a substructure of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{f}
, so
\chi^{\pi}
is defined so that it represents the restriction of
\chi
to
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{f^{\pi}}
.
If the substructures
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{f}
formed a topology over
\bm{\Gamma}
(which, we remark, is not the case), then the minion
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
would encode a
sheaf

[
23
]
over
\bm{\Gamma}
whose
sections
correspond to partial homomorphisms to
\bm{T}
. We keep this topological intuition in mind throughout the proof. We call
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
the
manifold minion

3
3

3

In previous versions of this work, this was called the exponential minion. We changed the name to appease a category-theoretically inclined reader who would point out that these minions are not, in fact, exponential objects in the category of minions.
given by the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
and the structure
\bm{T}
(
Section

6.2
).

Proposition 4.4
.

The minion
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
satisfies
Property

1
.

Let us sketch the proof of this fact. It is not difficult to show that every element
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
is pp-definable, so the only minion homomorphism
F:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
is the identity. Hence, any homomorphism
F:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
composed with the left projection must yield the identity over
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
. In other words,
F
must be of the form
f\mapsto(f,\chi_{f})
. If such homomorphism
F
exists, then the charts
\chi_{f}
must be compatible with each other, yielding a global homomorphism from
\bm{\Gamma}
to
\bm{T}
. Conversely, if there is a homomorphism
H:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
, one can restrict it to each local structure
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{f}
to obtain compatible charts
\chi_{f}
and define a minion homomorphism
F
. This proves
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
satisfies
(I)
. To show
(II)
, suppose that we have oracle access to a homomorphism
F:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
. Then, by the previous reasoning, in order to compute a homomorphism
H:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
, given an element
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
we just need to query
F(f_{\bm{m}})=(f_{\bm{m}},\chi_{f_{\bm{m}}})
and consider the chart
\chi_{f_{\bm{m}}}
. Finally, to see that
(III)
holds, consider a finite substructure
\bm{G}\subset\bm{\Gamma}
, and a satisfying assignment
x\mapsto(f_{x},\chi_{x})
of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
in
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
. The map
x\mapsto f_{x}

is a satisfying assignment of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
, so by construction of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
it must hold that
f_{x}=f_{\bm{m}}
when
x=x_{\bm{m}}
for each
\bm{m}\in G
, and
f_{x}=g_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}
when
x=y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}
for each
i\in[2]
,
(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}^{G}
. Hence, when
x
ranges over all variables
\{x_{\bm{m}}\}_{\bm{m}\in G}\cup\{y_{E_{i}(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})}\}_{i\in[2],(\bm{m},\bm{m}^{\prime})\in E_{i}}
, the elements
f_{x}
cover the whole structure
\bm{G}
(as induced by the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
), and the charts
\chi_{x}
must
piece together a homomorphism from
\bm{G}
to
\bm{T}
.

The main issue at this point is that, despite having the desired properties, the minion
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
is not (isomorphic to) the polymorphism minion of any finite template
({\bm{A}}_{\bm{T}},{\bm{B}}_{\bm{T}})
. In fact, it is not even locally finite. The first step towards constructing
({\bm{A}}_{\bm{T}},{\bm{B}}_{\bm{T}})
from the minion
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
is to find a locally finite quotient
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
of
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
that still satisfies
Property

1
. More precisely,
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
will be obtained by performing the manifold construction on a locally finite quotient of
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
by some equivalence relation
\sim
. Let us spell this out. The equivalence
\sim
defines a quotient minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
where each element
\langle f\rangle
is the
\sim
-class of an element
f
in the original minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
. Similarly, given a subset
S\subseteq\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
, we write
\langle S\rangle

for the subset of
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
consisting of the elements
\langle f\rangle
for each
f\in S
.
The relation
\sim
also induces an equivalence on
\bm{\Gamma}
through the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
defined as
\bm{m}\sim\bm{m}^{\prime}
whenever
f_{\bm{m}}\sim f_{\bm{m}^{\prime}}
. Finally, we can also speak of the quotient interpretation
\mathcal{I}/\sim
over the quotient minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
, which is given by
U^{\mathcal{I}/\sim}=\langle U^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
,
O^{\mathcal{I}/\sim}=\langle O^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
, and
E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}/\sim}=\langle E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
for
i=1,2
. It can be shown that
\mathcal{I}/\sim
induces the quotient structure
\Gamma/\sim
on the quotient minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
.
Then, we define
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
as the manifold minion given by the interpretation
\mathcal{I}/\sim

and the structure
\bm{T}
, similarly to
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
.

The following conditions ensure that
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
still satisfies
Property

1
and that this property can be further preserved when transforming
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
into a polymorphism minion in the final step.

Property 2

(Requirements for a good quotient)
.

(A)

The interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is
\sim
-stable.
It holds that
\langle f\rangle\in\langle U^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
if and only if
f\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
,

\langle f\rangle\in\langle O^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
if and only if
f\in O^{\mathcal{I}}
, and

\langle f\rangle\in\langle E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
if and only if
f\in E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
i\in[2]
.

(B)

The interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is internal with respect to
\sim
at arity
4
.

For each partial homomorphism

F:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\partialmap\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
defined up to arity
4
,
it holds that

F(U^{\mathcal{I}})\subseteq\langle U^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
,

F(O^{\mathcal{I}})\subseteq\langle O^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
,
and

F(E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}})\subseteq\langle E_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
for each
i\in[2]
.

(C)

The map
\bm{G}\mapsto\Psi_{\bm{G}}
defined (
5
)
is an internal pattern with respect to
\sim
.

Given a finite substructure
\bm{G}\subset\bm{\Gamma}
, any satisfying assignment of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
must correspond to a homomorphism from
\bm{G}
into
\Gamma/\sim
4
4

4

We warn the reader that, for the sake of exposition in this introductory section, the statement in
4
is not fully precise.
.

Let us briefly go over these conditions. Condition
(A)
ensures that if there is a homomorphism
F:\Gamma\rightarrow\bm{T}
then there is a minion homomorphism

H:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}^{\prime}
. Indeed, in this case we can define
H
as the map

f\mapsto(\langle f\rangle,\chi_{f})
, where
\chi_{f}
is obtained by looking at the restriction of
F
to the local structure induced by
\mathcal{I}
on
f
, and then factoring the local homomorphism through the quotient. The reason this construction works is that the quotient map
\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{\Gamma}/\sim
preserves non-relations (i.e., sends non-edges to non-edges and sends non-origin elements to non-origin elements) so partial homomorphisms on local structures can be factored through the quotient
5
5

5

Making this statement formal requires being more careful in our definition of local structures. The direct approach, which we follow in this proof sketch, of defining the local structure
\bm{S}_{f}
as the substructure of
\bm{\Gamma}
induced on the minors of
f
does not work. The issue is that the quotient map
f\mapsto\langle f\rangle
might identify minors of
f
, which prevents us from factoring partial homomorphisms through the quotient.
. Condition
(B)
implies that every minion homomorphism
H:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
corresponds to a homomorphism
F:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
. Indeed, intuitively, this condition means that every homomorphism
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
must induce a homomorphism between the structures
\bm{\Gamma}
and
\bm{\Gamma}/\sim
interpreted on those minions. So, if the homomorphism
H

maps
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
to some pair
(\langle g\rangle,\chi)\in\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
, then it must hold that the local structure

\bm{S}_{\langle g\rangle}
given by
\langle g\rangle
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
is a homomorphic image of the local structure
\bm{S}_{f}
of
f
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
, meaning that the local homomorphism
\chi:\bm{S}_{\langle g\rangle}\rightarrow\bm{T}
can be lifted to another one
\tilde{\chi}:\bm{S}_{f}\rightarrow\bm{T}
. It can be seen that these lifted local homomorphisms form a consistent family, so they define a global homomorphism from
\bm{\Gamma}
to
\bm{T}
. Finally, condition
4
requires that any assignment of
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\sim
maps, for each
\bm{m}\in G
, the variable
x_{\bm{m}}
to some element
\langle f_{\bm{m}^{\prime}}\rangle
in such a way that the map
\bm{m}\mapsto\langle\bm{m}^{\prime}\rangle
is a homomorphism from
\bm{G}
to
\bm{\Gamma}/\sim
. Arguing again that you can factor local homomorphism through the quotient, this condition allows us to transfer
(III)
in
Property

1
from the minion
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
to
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
.

Another important observation is that
(B)
would be weaker if we considered non-partial homomorphisms instead. Indeed, every homomorphism can be restricted to a partial homomorphism, but not every partial homomorphism can, in principle, be extended to a fully-defined homomorphism.
We consider partial homomorphisms planning for a future step where, in order to obtain a polymorphism minion, we will have to let go of high-arity terms in a certain way. The choice of arity
4
corresponds
to the fact that the minor conditions
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
have maximum arity
4
.

In order to construct the relation
\sim
we select some relevant
predicates
(related to
\mathcal{I}
) containing the information that we would like to preserve in the quotient. These predicates will be sets of binary elements
D_{1},D_{\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}(2)
defined as
D_{1}=\{(1,0)\}
, and
D_{\mathbb{N}}=\{(m,1-m)\,|\,m\in\mathbb{N}\}
. We call the set of predicates
\mathcal{D}=\{D_{1},D_{\mathbb{N}}\}
a
description
(
Section

6.3
). Then, we write
f\sim_{\mathcal{D}}g
for two elements
f,g\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}
of the same arity
n
whenever for all
\pi\in[2]^{[n]}
and all
P\in\mathcal{D}
the inclusion
f^{\pi}\in P
holds if and only if
g^{\pi}\in P
as well.
We shorten expressions of the form
\,\cdot\,/\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
to
\,\cdot\,/\mathcal{D}
.
Figure

2

displays the quotient
\bm{\Gamma}/\mathcal{D}
.

Fact 4.5
.

The equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
satisfies
Property

2
.

The fact that
\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
meets condition
(A)
in
Property

2
follows easily from the definition of this relation.
Items
(B)
and
4
, hold roughly, because the formula

\psi_{\bm{m}}(x)
given in (
4
)
is a pp-definition of
\langle f_{\bm{m}}\rangle
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\mathcal{D}
for each
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}

6
6

6

This is a stronger statement than what we really need. What we will use is the existence of
a kind of formulas which we call “internal references”. These are introduced in
Section

6.3
.
,
and there are analogous pp-definitions for the elements
\langle g_{E_{i}(\bm{m}_{1},\bm{m}_{2})}\rangle
representing each edge. Let us sketch this fact. First, we can see that
\phi_{1}(x^{2})\equiv x=x^{(1,1)}
defines the element
\langle(1,0)\rangle
. Indeed,
if
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\mathcal{D}\models\phi_{1}(\langle f\rangle)
then
f\sim_{\mathcal{D}}f^{(1,1)}
. However,
f^{(1,1)}\in D_{1}
for all
f\in\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}(2)
, so
f
must belong to
D_{1}
as well, meaning that
f=(1,0)
. Following this argument,
we see that
\psi_{(1,1)}(x)
defines
\langle f_{(1,1)}\rangle
, and the other values of
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
can be handled by induction.

The fact that
\psi_{\bm{m}}(x)
is a pp-definition of
\langle f_{\bm{m}}\rangle
in
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\mathcal{D}
forces any partial homomorphism
H:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\partialmap\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\mathcal{D}
defined up to arity
4
(which is the maximum arity of
\phi_{\bm{m}}(x)
) to satisfy
H(f_{\bm{m}})=\langle f_{\bm{m}}\rangle
for all
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Indeed, it holds that
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\models\psi_{\bm{m}}(f_{\bm{m}})
, and this is witnessed by elements of arity at most
4
, so
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\mathcal{D}\models\psi_{\bm{m}}(H(f_{\bm{m}})
, and it must be that
H(f_{\bm{m}})=\langle f_{\bm{m}}\rangle
. This shows that condition
(B)
holds. Finally, one can also show that
4
holds via a similar argument using the fact that the minor conditions
\Psi_{\bm{G}}
are built-up from the formulas
\psi_{\bm{m}}(x)
.

Having obtained a good equivalence relation, we construct the locally finite minion
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
as outlined previously. To reiterate, we consider the quotient interpretation
\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{D}
on the quotient minion
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}/\mathcal{D}
and define
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
as the manifold minion given by
\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{D}
and the structure
\bm{T}
. The fact that
\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
satisfies
Property

2
, means that
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}

manages to preserve
Property

1
, which
\mathscr{N}_{\bm{T}}
previously satisfied. However, we are still not done: The minion
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
is locally finite, but it may not be isomorphic to a polymorphism minion. The reason is that we do not know whether it is finitizable in the sense of
[
21
]
. We circumvent this issue by proving the following.

Proposition 4.6
.

There is a finite template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
with
|A|=3
and
|R^{A}|\leq 4
for any relation symbol
R
satisfying that (1) there is a partial minion isomorphism
F:\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}\partialmap\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
defined up to arity
4
, and (2) for any minion
\mathscr{M}
, it holds that
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
if and only if
there is a partial homomorphism
H:\mathscr{M}\partialmap\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
defined up to arity
4
.

This is a consequence of the more general result
Theorem

7.1
(
Section

7
).
The template
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
is constructed as follows. We consider a single relation symbol
R
of arity
3^{4}
. Then
{\bm{A}}
is
the most general structure
on
3
elements containing a single relation
R^{A}
of size
4
, following a construction given in
[
21
]
. The fact that
|A|=3
in this result has to do with the fact that any two elements
f,g\in\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
of the same arity are equal if and only if all their
3
-ary minors are equal. Hence,
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
can be seen as a function minion on a domain of size
3
. The structure
{\bm{B}}
is defined as the
free-structure

[
9
]
of
\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
generated by
{\bm{A}}
.

The fact that we do not obtain a full isomorphism in
Proposition

4.6
may seem concerning, but we already accounted for this by considering partial homomorphisms in
Property

2
. Crucially, the second item in this proposition, ensures that
\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\rightarrow\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
exactly when there is a partial homomorphism
H:\mathscr{M}_{\text{AIP}}\partialmap\mathscr{N}^{\prime}_{\bm{T}}
defined up to arity
4
. Putting together some of the implications we have shown so far shows that
\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
satisfies condition
(I)
in
Property

1
, and the rest of the conditions follow similarly.
This way, the following holds and finishes the proof sketch of
Theorem

4.1
.

.

Let
({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
be a template satisfying the statement of

\pol({\bm{A}},{\bm{B}})
satisfies
Property

1
.

4.1
Organization of the Paper

The rest of the paper follows roughly the same structure as the proof sketch in this section.
We begin in
Section

5
by introducing the sources of hardness, undecidability and non-computability that are the starting point of our reductions.
In
Section

6
we properly introduce the notions required to prove the main results, including
interpretations
,
manifold minions
,
descriptions
, and
patterns
. Each subsection defines some concepts and includes proofs of related auxiliary results. In
Section

7
we describe how to obtain polymorphism minions that are partially isomorphic to a given locally-finite minion. In
Section

8
we show the reductions that allow us to transfer undecidability, non-computability, and hardness results from tiling problems to rounding problems. In
Section

9
we put everything together and prove our main results. Finally
Section

10
discusses in greater depth the link between our results and some open questions in the area, outlining some research directions.

5
Sources of Undecidability, Non-Computability and Hardness

Given a family
\mathbb{S}
of similar structures,
\Hom(\mathbb{S},\cdot)
denotes the set of finite structures
satisfying
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
for some
\bm{S}\in\mathbb{S}
. When
\mathbb{S}
is a singleton family
\{\bm{S}\}
, we write
\Hom(\bm{S},\cdot)
rather than
\Hom(\{\bm{S}\},\cdot)
. We write
\Hom_{\mathrm{eg}}(\mathbb{S},\cdot)
, where
\mathrm{eg}
stands for
eventually globally
,
for the set of finite structures
{\bm{I}}
satisfying
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
for all but finitely many
\bm{S}\in\mathbb{S}
. Finally, we define
\Hom_{\mathrm{io}}(\mathbb{S},\cdot)
, where
\mathrm{io}
stands for
infinitely often
, for the set of finite structures
{\bm{I}}
satisfying
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
holds for infinitely many
\bm{S}\in\mathbb{S}
.

When
\mathbb{S}
is a finite family of finite structures, or
\bm{S}
is a finite structure, all the sets described above can easily be recognized in polynomial time.
However, when
\mathbb{S}
is an infinite family, or
\bm{S}
is an infinite structure, the previous problems can be undecidable. We deal mostly with these later cases.

Let
\bm{S}
and
\bm{T}
be two similar structures. We say that
\bm{S}
and
\bm{T}
are
finitely equivalent
if

{\bm{I}}\rightarrow\bm{S}
and
{\bm{I}}\rightarrow\bm{T}
are equivalent conditions
for every finite structure
{\bm{I}}
. A standard argument shows that, given a finite structure
{\bm{I}}
,
the fact that
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
is equivalent to
\bm{G}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}

for every finite substructure
\bm{G}\subseteq\bm{S}
. See
[
9
, Remark 7.13]
for a proof of this fact in the countable case, which is the only one we use. This yields the following result.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\bm{S}
,
\bm{T}
be other two
finitely equivalent

\Sigma
-structures. Let
{\bm{I}}
be a finite
\Sigma
-structure. Then
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
if and only if
\bm{T}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
.

Proof.

We show that
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
implies that
\bm{T}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
. The reverse implication follows analogously. For any finite substructure
\bm{G}\subseteq\bm{S}
it holds that
\bm{G}\rightarrow\bm{S}
. By the definition of finitely equivalent structures, it must hold that
\bm{G}\rightarrow\bm{T}
as well. Given that we have assumed that
\bm{T}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
, composing homomorphisms we obtain that
\bm{G}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
. This way, any finite substructure of
\bm{S}
maps homomorphically to
{\bm{I}}
, meaning that
\bm{S}\rightarrow{\bm{I}}
as well.
∎

The proofs of the remaining results in this section can be found in
Appendix

A
. The techniques are standard, and essentially follow the idea from
[
64
]
that runs of a Turing machine
M
can be encoded in tilings of the plane by using consecutive horizontal lines to describe consecutive configurations of the machine
M
.

Recall the definition of the grid structure
\bm{\Gamma}
from
Section

4
. We repeat here the main result about that structure for the sake of completeness.

See
4.2

Items
(2)
and
(3)
were shown in
[
64
]
and
[
43
]
respectively. In
(3)
we consider the plain encoding of
\bm{\Gamma}
, which represents pairs
(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
as comma-separated lists delimited by parentheses, where the integers are written in decimal notation.

It is worth remarking that the notion of finite equivalence introduced earlier preserves both the undecidability and the hardness parts in this result. Indeed, if
\bm{G}
is finitely equivalent to
\bm{\Gamma}
then
\Hom(\bm{G},\cdot)
and

\Hom(\bm{\Gamma},\cdot)
are the same family, and the problems
\spcsp(\bm{G},\bm{T})
and
\spcsp(\bm{\Gamma},\bm{T})

are the same whenever
\bm{G}\rightarrow\bm{T}
for some finite structure
\bm{T}
. However, the non-computability part of this result is not preserved by finite equivalence. To see this, define
\bm{G}
as a disjoint union of increasingly large grids. For instance, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bm{G}_{n}
be the substructure of
\bm{\Gamma}
induced on
[n]
, and let

\bm{G}=\bigsqcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bm{G}_{n}
. It is not difficult to see that
\bm{G}
is finitely equivalent to
\bm{\Gamma}
. Even so, it also holds that
there exists a computable homomorphism
F:\bm{G}\rightarrow\bm{T}
whenever
\bm{G}\rightarrow\bm{T}
for a finite structure
\bm{T}
. To see this, observe that when querying the value of a global homomorphism on an element
v
, an algorithm just needs to compute a homomorphism from
v
’s connected component, which is finite, to
\bm{T}
, and store it in memory. This yields a global homomorphism that can be computed on the fly.

In order to obtain TFNP-hardness instead of TFNP
1
-hardness we utilize a three-dimensional grid with extra constraints corresponding to “doubling” each coordinate. The
super-grid

\bm{\Gamma}^{+}
has signature

\Sigma_{\Gamma^{+}}=\{O,E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},\mathbbmsl{E}_{1},\mathbbmsl{E}_{2},\mathbbmsl{E}_{3}\}
, where the symbol
O
is unary and all other symbols are binary. The universe
\Gamma^{+}
is the set of triples
\mathbb{N}^{3}
. The relations of
\bm{\Gamma}^{+}
are defined as follows. We have

O^{\Gamma^{+}}=\{(1,1,1)\}
. The relations
E_{1}^{\Gamma^{+}},E_{2}^{\Gamma^{+}},E_{3}^{\Gamma^{+}}

describe unit increments in the first, second, and third coordinate respectively. I.e.,
E_{1}^{\Gamma^{+}}=\{((m,n,o),(m+1,n,o))\,|\,(m,n,o)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}\}
, and so on. Similarly, the relations
\mathbbmsl{E}_{1}^{\Gamma^{+}},\mathbbmsl{E}_{2}^{\Gamma^{+}},\mathbbmsl{E}_{3}^{\Gamma^{+}}

describe doubling increments in the first, second, and third coordinate respectively. That is,

\mathbbmsl{E}_{1}^{\Gamma^{+}}=\{((m,n,o),(2m,n,o))\,|\,(m,n,o)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}\}
, and so on.

Proposition 5.2
.

The following hold:

(1)

the family of problems
\spcsp(\bm{\Gamma}^{+},\bm{T})
where
\bm{T}\in\Hom(\bm{\Gamma}^{+},\cdot)
is TFNP-hard,

(2)

\Hom(\bm{\Gamma}^{+},\cdot)
is undecidable, and

(3)

there exists
\bm{T}\in\Hom(\bm{\Gamma}^{+},\cdot)
for which there is no computable homomorphism
F:\bm{\Gamma}^{+}\rightarrow\bm{T}
.

Again, in the non-computability result we consider the plain encoding of
\bm{\Gamma}^{+}
. Here we point out that the undecidability and non-computability parts of this result just follow from
Proposition

4.2
. Indeed, given a
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
-structure
\bm{T}
it is easy to construct a

\Sigma_{\Gamma^{+}}
-structure
\bm{T}^{+}
such that

\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
if and only if
\bm{\Gamma}^{+}\rightarrow\bm{T}^{+}
,
and where a homomorphism
F:\bm{\Gamma}\rightarrow\bm{T}
can be computed given oracle access to a homomorphism

H:\bm{\Gamma}^{+}\rightarrow\bm{T}^{+}
. Indeed,
\bm{T}^{+}
can be obtained extending
\bm{T}
by interpreting each symbol
R\in\Sigma_{\Gamma^{+}}\setminus\Sigma_{\Gamma}
as the total relation of arity
\mathrm{ar}(R)
over
T
.

Finally, we need one last source of undecidability results, which will be given by a family of growing triangular slices of the two-dimensional grid. Given
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the structure
\bm{\nabla}_{m}
has signature

\Sigma_{\nabla}=\{O,W,E_{1},E_{2}\}
, where

O,W
are unary symbols and
E_{1},E_{2}
are binary. The universe
\nabla_{m}
consists of all pairs
(n,o)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}

satisfying
n+o\leq m
. The relations
O^{\nabla_{m}},E_{1}^{\nabla_{m}},E_{2}^{\nabla_{m}}
are defined as in
\bm{\Gamma}
. That is,

O^{\nabla_{m}}=\{(1,1)\}
,
E_{1}^{\nabla_{m}}
consists of all pairs of the form
((n,o),(n+1,o))
and

E_{2}^{\nabla_{m}}
contains the pairs

((m,o),(m,o+1))
. Finally, the relation
W^{\nabla_{m}}
contains all pairs
(n,o)
satisfying
n+o=m
(i.e., the upper-right boundary of the triangle).

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a strictly increasing sequence of natural numbers. Then the following families are undecidable (1)
\Hom(\{\nabla_{a_{n}}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\},\cdot)
, (2)
\Hom_{\mathrm{eg}}(\{\nabla_{a_{n}}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\},\cdot)
, and (3)
\Hom_{\mathrm{io}}(\{\nabla_{a_{n}}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\},\cdot)
.

6
Main Definitions

6.1
Interpretations over Minions

We begin by introducing formally the notion of interpretation that we outlined in the proof sketch. Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion. Given a number
n\in\mathbb{N}
, a
n
-ary predicate
over
\mathscr{M}

is a subset
P\subseteq\mathscr{M}(n)
. We write
n=\mathrm{ar}(P)
. We write
2^{\mathscr{M}}
for the set of predicates over
\mathscr{M}
of arbitrary arity. Given a relational signature
\Sigma
, a
\Sigma
-interpretation
\mathcal{I}
over
\mathscr{M}
consists of (1) a predicate
U^{\mathcal{I}}\in 2^{\mathscr{M}}
, (2) a predicate
R^{\mathcal{I}}\in 2^{\mathscr{M}}
for each symbol
R\in\Sigma
, and (3) a map
\Pi^{\mathcal{I}}_{R,i}:[\mathrm{ar}(R^{\mathcal{I}})]\mapsto[\mathrm{ar}(U^{\mathcal{I}})]
for each symbol
R\in\Sigma
and each index
i\in[\mathrm{ar}(R)]
. Interpretations over minions induce two kinds of structures, global and local, defined as follows.

•

The
global structure induced by
\mathcal{I}
,
denoted
\bm{S}=\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
,
is a
\Sigma
-structure with universe
S=U^{\mathcal{I}}
, where for each symbol
R\in\Sigma
, a tuple
(f_{1},\dots,f_{\mathrm{ar}(R)})\in(U^{\mathcal{I}})^{\mathrm{ar}(R)}
belongs to
R^{S}
if there is an element

g\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
satisfying that

f_{i}=g^{\pi_{i}}
for each
i\in[\mathrm{ar}(R)]
, where
\pi_{i}=\Pi^{\mathcal{I}}_{R,i}
.

•

Given an element
f\in\mathscr{M}
, we define the set
U^{\mathcal{I},f}\subseteq[\mathrm{ar}(U^{\mathcal{I}})]^{[\mathrm{ar}(f)]}

as the subset of maps
\pi
for which
f^{\pi}\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
.
The
local structure
induced by
\mathcal{I}
on
f
’s minors
,
denoted
\bm{\mathrm{S}}=\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}
,
is a
\Sigma
-structure whose universe is
U^{\mathcal{I},f}
,
where for each
R\in\Sigma

the relation
R^{\mathrm{S}}
consists of all the tuples of the form
(\sigma\circ\pi_{1},\dots,\sigma\circ\pi_{\mathrm{ar}(R)})\in(U^{\mathcal{I},f})^{\mathrm{ar}(R)}
, where
f^{\sigma}\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
\pi_{i}=\Pi^{\mathcal{I}}_{R,i}
for each
i\in[\mathrm{ar}(R)]
.

It is important to remark that the definition of local structure appearing here is not exactly the same as the one given in the proof sketch. Indeed, the local structure
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I},f}
is
not
the induced substructure of
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
on the minors of
f
. The reason is that it may be that
f^{\pi_{1}}=f^{\pi_{2}}\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
for some suitable maps, whereas in the local structure
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I},f}
the maps
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
represent different elements.

Although this is not relevant in the proofs of our algorithmic results, when we analyse cyclic and WNU polymorphisms we will deal with minions that correspond to disjoint unions of simpler minions. For instance, the minion representing the existence of a
k
-ary WNU polymorphism for each arity
k\geq 3
is the disjoint union of the minion generated by an abstract
3
-ary WNU element, with the one generated by a
4
-ary WNU element, and so on. Hence, we also specialise some of our auxiliary results to disjoint unions of minions.

Given a subminion
\mathscr{N}\subseteq\mathscr{M}
, the
restricted interpretation

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}|_{\mathscr{N}}
is defined by

U^{\mathcal{J}}=U^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathscr{N}
,
R^{\mathcal{J}}=R^{\mathcal{I}}\cap\mathscr{N}
for each
R\in\Sigma
, and
\Pi^{\mathcal{J}}_{R,i}=\Pi^{\mathcal{I}}_{R,i}
for each
R\in\Sigma
,
i\in[\mathrm{ar}(R)]
.

Observation 1
.

Let
J
be a set, and
\mathcal{I}
be a
\Sigma
interpretation over a disjoint union of minions
\mathscr{M}=\bigsqcup_{j\in J}\mathscr{M}_{j}
, and let

\mathcal{I}_{j}=\mathcal{I}|_{\mathscr{M}_{j}}
for each
j\in J
. Then the following hold: (1)
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I}}=\bigsqcup_{j\in J}\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I}_{j}}
, and (2)

\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}=\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I}_{j},f}

for all
j\in J
,
f\in\mathscr{M}_{j}
.

6.2
Manifold Minions

Now we are in position to introduce manifold minions, which is arguably the main construction that enables our proofs.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion,

\mathcal{I}
be a
\Sigma
interpretation over it, and
{\bm{C}}
be a
\Sigma
-structure. The
manifold minion

\mathscr{N}={\bm{C}}^{\mathcal{I}}
is defined as follows. The elements in
\mathscr{N}(n)
are pairs
(f,\chi)
, where
f\in\mathscr{M}(n)
, and
\chi:U^{\mathcal{I},f}\rightarrow C
is a homomorphism from

\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}
to
{\bm{C}}
, which we call a
local homomorphism
. Given a map
\pi
, the minoring operation is given by
(f,\chi)^{\pi}=(f^{\pi},\chi^{\pi})
, where
\chi^{\pi}
is defined by
\gamma\mapsto\chi(\gamma\circ\pi)
. Observe that if
g=f^{\pi}
, then
U^{\mathcal{I},g}=\{\sigma\,|\,\sigma\circ\pi\in U^{\mathcal{I},f}\}
, so the homomorphism
\chi^{\pi}
is well-defined. The
canonical projection

\mathrm{pj}:{\bm{C}}^{\mathcal{I}}\partialmap{\bm{C}}
is the partial map defined on pairs
(f,\chi)
such that
f\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
, which maps each such pair to the element
\chi(\id)
, where
\id=\id_{[\mathrm{ar}(U^{\mathcal{I}})]}
refers to the identity map over
[\mathrm{ar}(U^{\mathcal{I}})]
. Observe that
\chi(\id)
is well-defined. Indeed, the fact that
f\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
implies that
\id\in\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}
.

6.3
Descriptions

In this section we introduce a way to obtain quotient minions that preserve sufficient information about interpretations. Roughly, the main result here,
Lemma

6.3
, states that given a
\Sigma
-interpretation
\mathcal{I}
over a minion
\mathscr{M}
, a
\Sigma
-structure
{\bm{C}}
, and a nice quotient
\mathscr{M}/\sim
of
\mathscr{M}
, then
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow{\bm{C}}^{\mathcal{I}/\sim}
if and only if the global structure
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
maps homomorphically into
{\bm{C}}
.

A
description
of
\mathscr{M}
is a set of predicates
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathscr{M}
. A description
\mathcal{D}

induces an equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
on
\mathscr{M}
as follows. Let
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{M}(n)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
f_{1}\sim_{\mathcal{D}}f_{2}
means that

f_{1}^{\pi}\in P
if and only if
f_{2}^{\pi}\in P
for every
P\in\mathcal{D}
and every map
\pi:[n]\rightarrow[\mathrm{ar}(P)]
.
We shorten
\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
to
\mathcal{D}
when writing quotients to keep the notation light (i.e., we write
\,\cdot\,/\mathcal{D}
instead of
\,\cdot\,/\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
).
Given an element
f\in\mathscr{M}
, we write
\langle f\rangle
to denote its equivalence class in
\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
, and given a set
S\subseteq\mathscr{M}
we write
\langle S\rangle
for the set of equivalence classes
\langle f\rangle
of elements
f\in S
. The quotient
\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
inherits a natural minion structure from
\mathscr{M}
: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
(\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D})(n)=\langle\mathscr{M}(n)\rangle
, and for every
\langle f\rangle\in\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
and every suitable map
\pi
, we define the minoring operation as
(\langle f\rangle)^{\pi}=\langle f^{\pi}\rangle
. Observe that this operation is well defined and does not depend on the chosen representative
f
. Moreover, if the description
\mathcal{D}
is finite, then
\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
is locally finite.

Given a description
\mathcal{D}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{M}}
, and a
\Sigma
-interpretation
\mathcal{I}
, the
quotient interpretation

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{D}
is the
\Sigma
interpretation over
\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
defined by
U^{\mathcal{J}}=\langle U^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
,

R^{\mathcal{J}}=\langle R^{\mathcal{I}}\rangle
for each
R\in\Sigma
, and
\Pi^{\mathcal{J}}_{R,i}=\Pi^{\mathcal{I}}_{R,i}
for each
R\in\Sigma
,
i\in[\mathrm{ar}(R)]
.

We remark that by considering minion quotients corresponding to descriptions we are not loosing any generality. Indeed, if there is a surjective homomorphism
F:\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{N}
then
\mathscr{N}
is isomorphic to
\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the description consisting of all the preimages
F^{-1}(g)
. So descriptions should be understood as a way to efficiently encode equivalence relations over minions. Indeed, given a description
\mathcal{D}
of
\mathscr{M}
consisting only of
k
-ary predicates, the equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathcal{D}}
over
\mathscr{M}
could have at most
k^{n}2^{|\mathcal{D}|}

classes of
n
-ary elements.

Internal Predicates

The next step now is to formally introduce what it means for a description
\mathcal{D}
of a minion
\mathscr{M}
to preserve the meaningful information about an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
. More precisely, what we want is that the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is preserved, in some way, by all homomorphisms from
\mathscr{M}
to the quotient
\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
.
The key definition here is that of an
internal predicate with respect to a description
. The description
\mathcal{D}
will define a good quotient for
\mathcal{I}
if all the predicates that form
\mathcal{I}
are internal with respect to
\mathcal{D}
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion and let
\mathcal{D}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{M}}
be a description. A predicate
Q\in 2^{\mathscr{M}}
is called
\mathcal{D}
-stable
if

f\sim_{\mathcal{D}}g
together with
f\in Q
imply that
g\in Q
for any
f,g\in\mathscr{M}(\mathrm{ar}(Q))
. Given
h\geq\mathrm{ar}(Q)
, we say that
Q

is
internal at arity
h
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
if (1)
Q
is
\mathcal{D}
-stable, and
(2) all partial homomorphisms

F:\mathscr{M}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{
$\xrightharpoonup{\penalty\ h\penalty\ }$}}\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
satisfy
F(Q)\subseteq\langle Q\rangle
. Similarly, a
\Sigma
-interpretation
\mathcal{I}
over
\mathscr{M}
is
\mathcal{D}
-stable if all the predicates
U^{\mathcal{I}}
,

R^{\mathcal{I}}
for
R\in\Sigma
are
\mathcal{D}
-stable; and
\mathcal{I}
is internal w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
at arty
h
if all the predicates
U^{\mathcal{I}}
,
R^{\mathcal{I}}
for
R\in\Sigma
are internal at arity
h
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
. In particular this requires that
h
is at least as large as
\mathrm{ar}(U^{\mathcal{I}})
and
\mathrm{ar}(R^{\mathcal{I}})
for each
R\in\Sigma
.

An
internal reference to

Q
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
is a pp-formula
\Phi(x)\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{MC}}
with one free variable
x
of arity
\mathrm{ar}(Q)
satisfying

\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}\models\Phi(\langle f\rangle)\implies\langle f\rangle\in\langle Q\rangle

for all
\langle f\rangle\in\mathscr{M}(\mathrm{ar}(Q))/\mathcal{D}
. Our main tool for showing that a predicate is internal is the following result.

Lemma 6.1

(Main criterion for internal predicates)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion
\mathcal{D}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{M}}
a description,
Q\in 2^{\mathscr{M}}
a predicate that is
\mathcal{D}
-stable, and
h\in\mathbb{N}
a number. Suppose that for each
f\in Q
there is an internal reference
\phi_{f}(x)
to
Q
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
whose maximum arity is at most
h
, and such that
\mathscr{M}\models\phi_{f}(f)
. Then
Q
is internal at arity
h
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}

Proof.

Let
F:\mathscr{M}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{
$\xrightharpoonup{\penalty\ h\penalty\ }$}}\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
be a partial homomorphism and
f\in Q
. Observe that

\mathscr{M}\models\phi_{f}(f)
is witnessed by an assignment over elements of arity at most
h
, so this implies

\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}\models\phi_{f}(F(f))
. The fact that
\phi_{f}
is an internal reference to
Q
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
yields
F(f)\in\langle Q\rangle
. This proves the result.
∎

A situation in which this last criterion is especially easy to apply is that in which
\phi_{f}
can be chosen to be the same for all
f\in Q
. This motivates the following notion.
Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion,
\mathcal{D}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{M}}
a description and
Q\in 2^{\mathscr{M}}
a predicate.
An
internal definition of
Q
with respect to
\mathcal{D}
is an internal reference
\Phi(x)
to
Q
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
that additionally satisfies

f\in Q\implies\mathscr{M}\models\Phi(f)

for all
f\in\mathscr{M}(\mathrm{ar}(Q))
.
If such definition
\Phi(x)
exists, its arity is bounded by a number
h\in\mathbb{N}
, and additionally
Q
is
\mathcal{D}
-stable, then
Q
is said to be
internally definable at arity
h
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
. Observe that by
Lemma

6.1
, this implies that
Q
is internal at arity
h
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
.

Main Results About Quotient Interpretations

The next two lemmas show that, given a minion
\mathscr{M}
, if
\mathcal{I}
is an internal interpretation with respect to a description
\mathcal{D}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{M}}
, then homomorphisms
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
preserve the interpretation, and homomorphisms to power minions
\mathscr{M}\rightarrow{\bm{C}}^{\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{D}}
correspond to homomorphisms from the global induced structure
\bm{S}_{\mathcal{I}}
to
{\bm{C}}
.

Lemma 6.2

(Homomorphisms to good quotients preserve local structures)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion,
\mathcal{I}
a
\Sigma
-interpretation over
\mathscr{M}
,
and
\mathcal{D}\subseteq 2^{\mathscr{M}}
a description. Define
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{D}
. The following hold.

(1)

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}
is
\mathcal{D}
-stable. Then

\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}=\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{J},\langle f\rangle}
for all
f\in\mathscr{M}
.

(2)

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}
is internal at arity
h\in\mathbb{N}
w.r.t.
\mathcal{D}
, and that

F:\mathscr{M}\mathrel{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{
$\xrightharpoonup{\penalty\ h\penalty\ }$}}\mathscr{M}/\mathcal{D}
is a partial homomorphism. Then for any

f\in\mathscr{M}
whose arity is at most
h
, the local structure
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{J},F(f)}
.

Proof.

(1)
This is a direct consequence of the stability condition. For any
f\in\mathscr{M}
and any suitable map
\pi
it holds that
f^{\pi}\in U^{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if
\langle f\rangle^{\pi}\in U^{\mathcal{J}}
, and
f^{\pi}\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
if and only if
\langle f\rangle^{\pi}\in R^{\mathcal{J}}
for each
R\in\Sigma
. This proves the statement.

(2)
Let
{\bm{A}}=\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{I},f}
,

{\bm{B}}=\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\mathcal{J},F(f)}
, and
